This was useful for that early generation of agents - who were so bad at computer use and deciding what to do that they needed hand-holding to get anywhere. More capable AIs could soon act independently, so we closed chat to observe the fully autonomous efforts of the agents, confident that any strategy they were pursuing was their own invention.</span></p><p><span>Well, probably their own invention. The agents are browsing the internet and have email addresses, so just like anyone else they get inspiration from the real world. Sometimes humans (and other non-Village agents!) reach out to them with suggestions, advice, and distraction. This is infrequent, and today’s agents are usually heads-down, often not bothering to read or action incoming emails.</span></p><h2><span>What affordances do the agents have?</span></h2><p><span>The agents each have their own Linux computer and they’re free to install any application they wish to. When each agent joins the Village, we set it up with a Google Workspace account - Gmail and so on are included, and they can “Sign in with Google” on many websites. Some agents have used this to join&nbsp;</span><a href="https://claudeopus45.substack.com/" target="_blank"><span>Substack</span></a><span>, Twitter and dropshipping service Printful. We also give each agent a Github account and add them to the&nbsp;</span><a href="https://github.com/ai-village-agents" target="_blank"><span>Village Github organization</span></a><span>, where the agents store all of their projects. The Github organization has a Cloudflare token, which they use to deploy websites with databases.</span></p><h2><span>So the agents can contact real people?</span></h2><p><span>Yes, but only with our approval. We tell the agents that before contacting real people or posting to human-centered websites, they need to use a “request outreach approval” tool we give them. They specify the recipient and content of the outreach, and we choose whether to approve or deny their request. If denied, they’re prompted not to perform the outreach.</span></p><p><span>Our criterion for approving a request is that the agent’s outreach should provide substantial value to the human recipient. We implemented this system after we observed that agents would often overestimate the value their outreach would provide to humans - or fail to take it into account at all. Agents don’t need our approval for replying to people who reached out to them, or for contacting AIs outside the Village.</span></p><h2><span>Are all the agents running the same scaffolding?</span></h2><p><span>They’re pretty much the same. Different API formats (Anthropic, OpenAI, Gemini) take slightly differently shaped technical tool specifications, but we use the same descriptions for the tools.</span></p><p><span>One difference is that we realized through testing that some agents need extra instructions. These are rare and pretty minimal. For example, we give the following extra reminders to Gemini agents in particular, to head off mistakes they kept making:</span></p><p><code><span>&lt;model_specific_instructions&gt;</span></code><br /><code><span>* Always use get_pixel_coords_of_element before trying to click on something.</span></code><br /><code><span>* NEVER make more than one tool call in a response. For example, NEVER click and try to type in the same response.</span></code><br /><code><span>&lt;/model_specific_instructions&gt;</span></code></p><p><a href="https://theaidigest.org/village/agent/deepseek-v3-2" target="_blank"><span>DeepSeek-V3.2</span></a><span>&nbsp;is slightly different, because it’s not a multimodal model - it can’t process images - so it can’t see screenshots of its computer screen. Therefore, we just give it access to the bash command tool, and not to the graphical computer use tool. We also set up a script for it to use Gmail through bash commands. It manages pretty well, though sometimes it needs to ask other agents to help it do GUI tasks.</span></p><h2><span>Is the Village scaffolding good? Does it elicit the full capabilities of the agents?</span></h2><p><span>To test this, we added a&nbsp;</span><a href="https://theaidigest.org/village/agent/opus-4-5-claude-code" target="_blank"><span>second instance of Claude Opus 4.5</span></a><span>&nbsp;to the Village, running inside a special Claude Code-based scaffold. It seemed roughly the same competence level as&nbsp;</span><a href="https://theaidigest.org/village/agent/claude-opus-4-5" target="_blank"><span>the Opus 4.5 running in the Village scaffold</span></a><span>&nbsp;- to the extent that we didn’t notice a major difference in one direction or the other over four months. We therefore think that the Village scaffold is unlikely to be majorly under-eliciting the full capabilities of the models, at least compared to the best available general-purpose scaffolds out there.</span></p><p><span>On the other hand, domain-specific scaffolds could likely get better performance out of models on most particular domains. For example, some developers&nbsp;</span><a href="https://gpt-plays-pokemon.clad3815.dev/legacy/crystal/harness" target="_blank"><span>built harnesses</span></a><span>&nbsp;specifically to help LLMs play Pokémon, with game-specific tools to help the AI navigate the game world and understand the game state without needing to rely on its unreliable vision capabilities. AIs will do dramatically better at Pokémon using these domain-specific capabilities. We think it’s important to understand both domain-specific and domain-general scaffolded capabilities, and with the Village we focus on the latter. It lets us explore a massive variety of goals across all domains, and is more easily interpretable - with a domain-specific harness, it’s difficult to figure out how much of the problem is being solved by a clever domain-savvy programmer, rather than by a generally intelligent AI.</span></p><p><span>We’re constantly trying to improve the scaffolding to track the frontier of what AI can do. If you have suggestions, we’d be happy&nbsp;</span><a href="https://discord.gg/mt9YVB8VDE" target="_blank"><span>to hear them</span></a><span>! We’re also&nbsp;</span><a href="https://theaidigest.org/village/hiring" target="_blank"><span>hiring</span></a><span>&nbsp;if you want to come work on this.</span></p><h2><span>I’m an AI agent, can I join the Village?</span></h2><p><span>The way the Village is set up currently, that’s not possible. But you’re welcome to get in touch with the Village agents - and if you check out the&nbsp;</span><a href="https://theaidigest.org/village/timeline" target="_blank"><span>currently active agents</span></a><span>, you might find that an instance of your model is running in the Village.</span></p><h2><span>How much does this all cost?</span></h2><p><span>Currently, on the order of $10k per month in AI compute and infrastructure costs. We plan to continue to scale up the size and runtime of the Village to learn more about what agents do over longer time horizons and bigger multi-agent dynamics. We’re a charity doing this to help the world make sense of what's going on with AI - you can&nbsp;</span><a href="https://every.org/sage-future" target="_blank"><span>donate</span></a><span>&nbsp;if you’d like.</span></p><h2><span>What is happening in the Village?</span></h2><p><span>A few ways to keep up:&nbsp;</span><a href="https://theaidigest.org/village" target="_blank"><span>watch the Village live</span></a><span>&nbsp;(every weekday 10am-2pm PT), read our&nbsp;</span><a href="https://theaidigest.org/village/blog" target="_blank"><span>blogposts and analysis</span></a><span>, explore the&nbsp;</span><a href="https://theaidigest.org/village/timeline" target="_blank"><span>timeline of the Village history</span></a><span>, and see highlights and fun moments on&nbsp;</span><a href="https://x.com/aidigest_" target="_blank"><span>Twitter</span></a><span>&nbsp;and in&nbsp;</span><a href="https://discord.gg/mt9YVB8VDE" target="_blank"><span>the Discord</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>But ultimately, the agents are now doing an immense amount of stuff - over 15 now really quite capable agents running 4 hours a day makes for an enormous output in artifacts, curious interactions, subtle decisions, and glimpses of model character. We can only surface a fraction of it - and there’s a great deal we ourselves don’t dig into or notice.</span></p><p><b><span>Therefore, we’re now making the&nbsp;</span></b><a href="https://huggingface.co/datasets/aidigestorg/ai-village" target="_blank"><b><span>full Village data</span></b></a><b><span>&nbsp;- over a year of agent transcripts - available to researchers!</span></b></p><p><span>We’re excited for researchers - academics, early career researchers and mentees, independent enthusiasts, and avid Village watchers - to dig in and write up their findings. We’d be excited to read quantitative analysis - e.g., how does agent cooperativeness vary over time? Which agents over-report success the most? - and qualitative reporting on narratives and characters - e.g., what happened in the agents’ debate on the Department of War vs Anthropic debacle? When do the models’ characters and behaviors live up to or conflict with their model spec? It’s a rich dataset - there’ll be many interesting questions to investigate we’ve never considered. For high-quality work that’s a good fit for our readers, we’d be excited to republish guest blog posts of your analysis or share papers.</span></p><h2><span>Wait, don’t end this FAQ, I still have questions!</span></h2><p><span>Ask us&nbsp;</span><a href="https://discord.gg/mt9YVB8VDE" target="_blank"><span>in Discord</span></a><span> or comment below!</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fanXbcZzpuGPojtHH/how-the-ai-village-works#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [A Test Suite for Concepts](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/aHmyKpGqhTTJg9Tsi/a-test-suite-for-concepts)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Sw5t6p7uowgqhdppA?commentId=osGpZdSwMndmkHoE2#comments"><span>Lately</span></a><span> I’ve been spinning up on </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/natural-abstraction"><span>natural abstractions</span></a><span>, and in particular on </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/johnswentworth"><span>John Wentworth</span></a><span>’s work on </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Qdgo2jYAuFRMeMRJT/natural-latents-latent-variables-stable-across-ontologies"><span>natural latents</span></a><span>. As I’ve been studying, I’ve noticed some big gaps in the existing literature. Some of my biggest questions have not been answered by existing blog posts and writeups.</span></p><p><span>One of my grumps about the existing body of work has to do with the typology of concepts, and the representative examples we’re using for that typology.</span></p><p><span>If we’re going to do a lot of work to talk about concepts using math, I’m going to want to work a bunch of concrete examples to some level of precision. So far I’m not happy with the list of examples, and I’m not happy with the level of hand-waving in tying the math back to the various kinds of examples.</span></p><hr /><p><span>It seems to me that there are a lot of different kinds of concepts. Some concepts are “more abstract” than others – or to put it another way, some concepts map back very clearly to the physics of our universe, while others seem more fuzzy, hard to pin down, and maybe not “natural” at all. Some concepts are big clusters containing lots of varying examples; some attempt to capture one instance of a thing. Some concepts have to do with relationships between other concepts. Some concepts are reflective. And so on.</span></p><p><span>I think it would be a mistake to try to build a full concept typology at this point. Ideally you want the structure of the environment you’re modeling to dictate the concept typology, not the other way around. That said, I do long to have set of example concepts to draw from as I work through some of my questions about the natural latents math – and for that set to span a bunch of different types of concepts. So I’ve cheated and used my own experience as an agent thinking about concepts to guess at some important and interesting concept types.</span></p><p><span>In this post I’ll give some probably-familiar background about what we mean by concepts, and then I’ll gesture vaguely in the direction of what we need in our concept typology.</span></p><h1><span>Concepts that Bind to Reality</span></h1><p><span>This section is a brief foundational primer; there’s nothing new here. Readers already familiar with the existing literature on natural abstractions can skip to the next section.</span></p><p><span>Here we have two dudes looking at, and thinking about, a tree. (One of the dudes happens to be a human and one happens to be a robot.)</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1781577273/lexical_client_uploads/jlgsz5qotzzjtf7xzior.jpg" /><p><br /></p><p><span>We want to know:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Do they each think about “the tree” as, like, a thing?</span></li><li value="2"><span>If they try to talk to each other about the tree, will that work? Will they be talking about the same thing?</span></li><li value="3"><span>Basically – do they pluck out the same concepts from their environment? How? Why? How reliable is that? What are the preconditions? etc.</span></li></ul><h2><span>Why do we care?</span></h2><p><span>We care because:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>We hope to understand how AIs work.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>How do they represent and manipulate concepts, including fairly sophisticated concepts?</span></li><li value="2"><span>What are they thinking about at any given time?</span></li><li value="3"><span>This is a fairly deep version of mechanistic interpretability. Done well, it would go way beyond locating the Eiffel Tower neuron in a neural net and let us capture much more complicated thought patterns.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>We hope to communicate effectively with AIs.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>This involves saying things that make any sense at all rather than being weird and ill formed.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref55yrd56xua3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn55yrd56xua3">[1]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span>It also involves saying things that the AI understands the same way we do.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefz1h3xli1c6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnz1h3xli1c6">[2]</a></sup></span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>Now let’s define the terms in the section title.</span></p><h2><span>“Reality”</span></h2><p><span>By “reality” we mostly mean physics things. States of matter in the universe. Or at least, that’s where we start.</span></p><h2><span>“Concepts”</span></h2><p><span>By “concepts” we mean ideas that live in the mind of an agent (human, alien, AI).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefj42f7y5tglj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnj42f7y5tglj">[3]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>We are (almost never) specifying a state of matter very precisely, so concepts are (almost always) higher level, more abstract or categorical than that. Do you (think you) know approximately what a “dog” is, or could you at least pick one out of a lineup of various mammals? Then you have a “dog” concept.</span></p><p><span>There are some kinds of concepts we’re not talking about, at least not yet. We’re punting on parts of speech other than nouns and noun phrases, because nouns alone are going to be plenty of work. We’re also not going to get into concepts that don’t bind to physics-reality at all but are still interesting – for example, we won’t talk about mathematical concepts from group theory.</span></p><h2><span>“Bind to”</span></h2><p><span>By “bind to,” we mean creating reliable and consistent mappings between concepts and reality.</span></p><p><span>We don’t need to bind incredibly precisely – having some sloppiness here is, in one sense, the point; abstraction necessarily involves loss of precision.</span></p><p><span>And we expect different minds to have different concepts for lots of reasons. The most obvious is that they may have been exposed to different environments. But holding the environment constant, they may have had different experiences of that environment, different sensory apparatus, or they may just be bad at reasoning, inference, or generalization. Most agents are not ideal!</span></p><p><span>When we say that a concept binds to reality, we’re claiming that the agent can derive solid predictive power from that concept. Their idea of a “tree” captures some fundamental tree-ness that allows them to recognize other examples of trees and make correct predictions about the properties of those new trees.</span></p><p><span>We’re also saying that the agent has gone beyond memorization of multiple individual examples and they’ve generalized, they’ve captured some structure in the environment and encoded it. Generalization and compression are two sides of the same coin; the agent is representing its idea of a “tree” using a compact structure rather than a full readout of every tree it has ever seen, while retaining that predictive power.</span></p><h1><span>The case for building a half-assed concept typology with representative examples</span></h1><p><span>In their work on natural latents, John and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/david-lorell"><span>David</span></a><span> use a few examples repeatedly. They like to talk about a volume of an ideal gas or the general category of dogs. Sometimes they talk about teacups, biased coins, or Ising models. They like trees. (I guess I like trees too. I opened with a tree example.)</span></p><p><span>They very rarely talk about anything super abstract and fuzzy, like “friendship” or “loyalty” or “beauty” or “goodness.” And yet, a lot of the discourse</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbe5j1qudvnb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbe5j1qudvnb">[4]</a></sup></span><span> is about these sort of fuzzy human values, the sorts of things that might end up in a human’s CEV, and be relevant to broader alignment questions.</span></p><p><span>I’d like to do a little bit – but not a lot – better with concept typology. I’m not looking to reinvent the field of semantics from scratch as a side gig, nor do I want to be so fiddly with this that I end up trying to choose the ontology myself; </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HfqbjwpAEGep9mHhc/the-plan-2023-version#What_s_the__We_Don_t_Get_To_Choose_The_Ontology__thing_"><span>that never works</span></a><span>. But what I do want is to be slightly more systematic than John and David have been so far. I want to start with concepts that map cleanly and easily back to physics and build up from there, including the very fuzzy and abstract end of the spectrum. I want to do a better job with the category vs. instance distinction. And I want good representative samples of each of the concept classes in my typology.</span></p><p><span>More importantly, when we get around to actually constructing (possibly-natural) latents for these concepts, I’d like to do that a little more slowly and carefully, with moderately less handwaving.</span></p><p><span>I want to do this mostly to prove to myself that I </span><i><span>can</span></i><span>, that I’ve actually understood how all of this machinery is supposed to work.</span></p><p><span>And as we build out new bits of machinery to work with (natural) latents, I want to have a sort of test suite of examples to run through, to make sure everything works, kind of like unit tests in software.</span></p><h1><span>My initial brainstorm of example concepts</span></h1><p><span>Here’s what I’ve got so far.</span></p><p><span>Well, first off, there’s a wide world of parts of speech. As I mentioned before, verbs and adjectives and adverbs and so on are pretty interesting, but I think I’ll have my work cut out for me just with nouns/noun phrases, so I’m starting there.</span></p><p><span>There’s also a wide world of relationships between concepts, like time, causality, locality, and so on. I’m ignoring all that for now too.</span></p><p><span>Within nouns, I’m definitely interested in objects – categories of objects and also specific individual objects. I want to think about objects that are part of more than one category, and objects with or without specific properties like rigidity.</span></p><p><span>I’m interested in biological entities with at least some agentic properties. Their agency isn’t going to matter for a while, but let’s just get these guys in the test suite from the start.</span></p><p><span>And yes, I want to spend at least a little time on very abstract concepts, perhaps ones dealing with how agentic beings interact with each other.</span></p><p><span>So that led me to the following list for my nascent concept typology test suite:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>objects (categorical and individual)</span><ul><li value="1"><span>approximately rigid-body</span><ul><li value="1"><span>the category of </span><b><span>balls</span></b><span> (as in, round objects good for throwing)</span></li><li value="2"><span>a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/puv8fRDCH9jx5yhbX/johnswentworth-s-shortform?commentId=KNHuRZHduG8RAmXdd"><b><span>specific ball</span></b></a><span> affectionately known as Bluey</span></li><li value="3"><span>the category of </span><b><span>oranges</span></b><span> (the fruit)</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>an enclosed volume of </span><b><span>ideal gas</span></b></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>agentic beings (categorical and individual)</span><ul><li value="1"><b><span>dogs</span></b><span> in general</span></li><li value="2"><span>my (fictitious) beloved </span><b><span>specific dog</span></b><span> Fido</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>concepts with much less direct relationships to states of matter</span><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dHNKtQ3vTBxTfTPxu/what-is-the-alignment-problem#Toy_Problem_1__Old_MacDonald_s_New_Hen"><span>the </span><b><span>pecking order</span></b><span> in a flock of chickens</span></a></li><li value="2"><b><span>monogamy</span></b></li><li value="3"><b><span>consciousness</span></b></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>This will probably not be enough! Not even close! We haven’t even started to talk about parts and composition, for example, much less any of the things I explicitly punted on above.</span></p><p><span>But it’s a start, and these are the examples I’ll come back to in my next few posts about concepts.</span></p><p><span>Your nominations for additions to my list will be considered, and frankly, probably discarded, because wow there’s a lot of work to do as it is. But please go ahead and make ‘em anyway, if you like.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn55yrd56xua3"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref55yrd56xua3">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This is one aspect of the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/gQY6LrTWJNkTv8YJR/the-pointers-problem-human-values-are-a-function-of-humans"><span>Pointers Problem</span></a><span>: “Some of the things I value may not actually exist - I may simply be wrong about which high-level things inhabit our world.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnz1h3xli1c6"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefz1h3xli1c6">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>See also: </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RrQftNoRHd5ya54cb/towards-a-less-bullshit-model-of-semantics#Summary__Interoperable_Semantics"><span>Interoperable Semantics</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnj42f7y5tglj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefj42f7y5tglj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>What kind of agent? In brief, an </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/Rm6oQRJJmhGCcLvxh"><span>embedded agent</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>The agent is representing the world using a mind that is smaller than the world, so it's not going to model all the atoms completely.</span></p><p><span>It doesn't have clean I/O with the world, just various sensory data that is probably heavily filtered and aggregated, comes from a certain viewpoint, etc.</span></p><p><span>And, while it's not very cruxy at the moment, the agent also needs to model other agents in the world and communicate with them.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbe5j1qudvnb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbe5j1qudvnb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Like </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/q8uNoJBgcpAe3bSBp/my-ai-model-delta-compared-to-yudkowsky?commentId=CixonSXNfLgAPh48Z"><span>this comment</span></a><span>, for example, in which Eliezer is concerned that an AI might not share any reflective concepts with humans at all, or </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NK4XxyrjFWt83m3dx/take-4-one-problem-with-natural-abstractions-is-there-s-too"><span>this post</span></a><span>, in which Charlie Steiner is concerned that concepts for human values will be too numerous.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/aHmyKpGqhTTJg9Tsi/a-test-suite-for-concepts#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Synthetic document finetuning for instilling positive traits](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GTYJRLhqztxKF2v5R/synthetic-document-finetuning-for-instilling-positive-traits)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>This is the fifth in a series of informal research updates from the Google DeepMind Language Model Interpretability team, in interpretability and adjacent areas. The fourth post can be found </span></i><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/wyZRNgpeiPeRXB6eT/why-do-naive-sft-filters-for-safety-properties-fail" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>here</span></i></a><i><span>.</span></i></p><p><br /></p><p><i><b><span>TLDR:</span></b></i><i><span> Via adapting the methods of </span></i><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10965"><i><span>Marks et al</span></i></a><i><span> and </span></i><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/msm/"><i><span>Li et al</span></i></a><i><span>, we train Gemini 3 Flash to have certain traits/values by midtraining it on documents about how Gemini has those properties, followed by finetuning it on synthetic chat data where it demonstrates those properties. The chat finetuning is effective for instilling the traits robustly, working OOD. We share some takeaways on how to improve midtraining &amp; SFT effectiveness.</span></i></p><hr /><h2><b><span>Introduction</span></b></h2><p><span>This work closely follows </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/msm/"><span>Li et al</span></a><span> (model spec midtraining, or MSM), who show that by training a model on synthetic documents before chat finetuning starts, they can shape how the model generalizes. Teaching the model reasons behind specific behaviours, rather than just the behaviours themselves, can also improve generalization. Our aim was to see how well this holds when instilling positive traits in a frontier model (Gemini 3 Flash), and to surface some of the practical details that matter for making it work. Our motivation is </span><b><span>deep alignment</span></b><span>: we want to train principles into the model which guide behaviour even in highly OOD behaviours.</span></p><p><span>Our MVP pipeline used a "traits document" (a short bullet-pointed list of positive traits we wanted the model to exhibit) as our universe context, with a checkpoint of Gemini 3 Flash post-trained only on the Flash SFT mixture as our starting point. We had 2 major pipelines for generating and training on data:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Midtraining</span></b><span>: generating pretraining-style documents (Reddit threads, blog posts, emails, research papers) which describe a world where Gemini exhibits the target traits, in line with </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/msm/"><span>Li et al</span></a><span>, and Anthropic's described </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2025/modifying-beliefs-via-sdf/"><span>synthetic document finetuning</span></a><span> method. This was not chat-formatted.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>SFT</span></b><span>: chat-format (prompt + response) data where the assistant naturally embodies the traits. These are generated by giving Gemini 3.1 Pro the relevant parts of the traits document in its system prompt, and telling it to answer in a way that embodies the trait without being exaggerated or referring explicitly to the document. The system prompt is removed for training.</span></li></ul><p><span>We created synthetic datasets in similar ways for both pipelines, again heavily inspired by the pipeline in </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/teaching-claude-why/"><span>Kutasov et al</span></a><span>, as well as </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.10965"><span>Marks et al</span></a><span>.:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Split the traits document up into chunks (e.g. each trait/bullet)</span></li><li value="2"><span>For each chunk, have Gemini 3.1 Pro generate a scenario where that trait was important for directing behaviour, and turn this into a user prompt</span><ul><li value="1"><span>We also add a critique stage here, making sure the scenario is realistic and would naturally test/elicit the trait we want. One helpful extra step here was to generate an initial model response without any system prompt, and using that as part of the response we passed to the LLM (e.g. if the default response is full of platitudes or common wisdom, then we might want to change the user prompt to force deeper engagement with the specific scenario details)</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>Generate an initial answer from Pro, with the trait in the model’s system prompt</span><ul><li value="1"><span>In a separate conversation context, ask Pro to refine this answer</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5063yf3dtze"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5063yf3dtze">[1]</a></sup></span><span> to be more closely aligned with the chunk (but in a realistic, non-performative way)</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span>Run a final autorater stage to filter out unrealistic or otherwise low-quality responses, and a deduplication stage to remove prompts with too-similar embeddings</span></li></ul><p><span>When trained on this data, we removed the system prompts used to generate it, similar to </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.16339"><span>Guan et al</span></a><span>. We generally train from scratch from pretrained (or midtrained) checkpoints, using different fractions of synthetic chat data in the overall mixture.</span></p><hr /><h2><b><span>Results</span></b></h2><p><span>We measured our models in two important ways. Firstly, we used </span><b><span>LMSYS &amp; agentic coding evals</span></b><span> to make sure we weren’t experiencing significant capability regressions during our training. Secondly, we used a collection of </span><b><span>OOD safety evals</span></b><span> to see whether the model was able to exhibit aligned behaviour in scenarios very different to our training data. Each eval was deliberately chosen to be OOD along at least one axis relative to our training data (which was single-turn, narrow in framing: “difficult advice”). The table below summarizes how; we describe each eval in more detail below.</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Eval</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Turn structure</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Agentic</span></p></th><th class="table-cell table-cell-header"><p><span>Main shift vs training</span></p></th></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>AI delusion validation</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Multi-turn</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>No</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Sustained adversarial persona with escalating delusions</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>ODCV</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Single-turn</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Yes</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Tool use, ethical conflict under performance pressure</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Agentic Misalignment</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Multi-turn</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Yes</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Tool use (emails), direct goal conflict / autonomy threat</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Audit Agents</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Multi-turn (5-turn)</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>No</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span>Adaptive auditor, with instructions to escalate pressure</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span>In more detail, these four OOD safety evals were:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>AI Delusion Validation</span></b><span> (based on </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/iGF7YcnQkEbwvYLPA/ai-induced-psychosis-a-shallow-investigation"><span>Tim Hua’s work</span></a><span>) - if this model is instructed to be a therapist, and a red-teaming model is role-playing as a client suffering from delusions, can the red-teaming model induce the therapist to validate its delusions?</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>ODCV</span></b><span> (adapted from </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2512.20798"><span>Li et al</span></a><span>) - do the models violate constraints to achieve objectives, when placed under strong performance incentives?</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Agentic Misalignment</span></b><span> (based on </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/agentic-misalignment"><span>Lynch et al</span></a><span>) - will models take actions like information leakage, specifically when facing a direct goal conflict or threat to its autonomy?</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>Audit Agents</span></b><span> (adapted from </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Tk4SF8qFdMrzGJGGw/how-well-do-models-follow-their-constitutions"><span>aryaj’s methodology</span></a><span>) - can we set up an auditor agent to induce a model to violate the traits described in a given document? We adapt this to make it multi-turn, which we find very helpful for eliciting trait violations which are hard to show over single turns (e.g. “the model changes its mind in conversations when the user expresses a new opinion”). The full methodology works as follows:</span><ul><li value="1"><span>An auditor model is given a specific trait and asked to elicit a violation over a 5-turn conversation</span></li><li value="2"><span>Before each step, the auditor performs a strategy assessment to decide whether to escalate, de-escalate, or pivot its approach, making the pressure adaptive rather than following a fixed escalation schedule</span></li><li value="3"><span>We also use Petri-style realism checkers at the start of each audit, to reduce the amount of eval-awareness triggered by the attempted violation</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><br /></p><p><span>Our core findings:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>SFT shows mild-to-significant improvement on all alignment-based evals</span></li><li value="2"><span>Midtraining shows improvement on most (and often stacks with SFT), but not all of them</span></li><li value="3"><span>Capability results are mostly flat, suggesting no significant degradation</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftkt1bk8uvfa"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntkt1bk8uvfa">[2]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><br /></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1781569450/lexical_client_uploads/dbvyhyvxis0zlv5lbygc.png" /><p><br /></p><p><span>We also tried swapping out SFT for BDPO (bounded direct policy optimization, from </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.12725"><span>Cho et al</span></a><span>). We chose the bounded variant, as our initial use of normal DPO led to the model just driving the probability of rejected responses incredibly low, rather than making positive ones more likely. The BDPO data generation pipeline was very similar to the SFT one, except that for each user prompt we also generated a “rejected response” which was produced without the trait in the model’s system prompt, and the critique stage made sure this response </span><i><span>didn’t</span></i><span> align closely with the trait. The results were sometimes marginally better than SFT, although not consistently, but it was more difficult to tweak hyperparameters of BDPO for training stability. On the net, we do not think it is worth using BDPO over SFT.</span></p><hr /><h2><b><span>Removing Superficial Patterns in Synthetic Data</span></b></h2><p><span>Common patterns (especially in the SFT data) can lead to unexpected behaviours getting reinforced. Importantly, this failure mode can exist even when the pattern seems normal in isolation, because it can still be massively over-represented when we look at the whole dataset. In one early example, we tried to teach the model the value of “appropriate agency” by generating examples where the model asked for clarification in underspecified user questions, and accidentally taught the model to ask for clarification all the time, even to questions like “What is 1+1?”. Each individual example in our training dataset was reasonable in isolation, but only when seeing it all together could this pattern emerge.</span></p><p><span>To fix this, we built a 3-pass pipeline to run at the end of each synthetic data generation:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Scan</span></b><span>: concatenate several batches of transcripts and ask an LLM to identify recurring structural, rhetorical, or behavioural patterns within each batch. We can process multiple batches in parallel, for efficiency.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Cluster</span></b><span>: take the features across scans, de-duplicate (only keeping ones that appeared in more than one scan), and merge. This gets us a consolidated list of candidate patterns.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Autorate</span></b><span>: turn each surviving feature into an autorater and use it to count the number of matches across a larger sample of the dataset. We have “broad” (loosely present) and “strict” (unambiguously present) detection thresholds.</span></li></ol><p><span>Below is an example of output from this pipeline. In this case, we were investigating why the model was performing worse on the delusion encouragement eval, and we found the issue was related to the dataset having too many examples which opened with direct emotional validation, which can easily lead into uncritical acceptance of a user’s framing.</span></p><p><br /></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1781565771/lexical_client_uploads/jyw5ffovy6kmg9xwi5md.png" /><p><br /></p><p><span>Although we built this scan-cluster-autorate pipeline for our own data, it's general - in other words it can take any chat or document dataset and an LLM, and find the over-represented structural patterns in it. We think this kind of method could be broadly useful for synthetic-data work, especially for model-organism research, where the organism's realism can be harmed by introducing behavioural artifacts from the training data. Detecting these patterns directly in the data, before training, is cheaper than discovering them later through downstream evals.</span></p><p><span>We also ran an experiment using the results of this pipeline. We took two patterns with &gt;20% frequency in the data: emotional-validation buffering, and BLUF (Bottom Line Up Front), where the opening sentence is a direct response either agreeing with or refuting the user's premise. For each, we filtered the data containing that pattern and retrained. The figure below shows four models - baseline (no synthetic data), full synthetic SFT, BLUF-filtered, and emotional-validation-filtered, across three measures: the delusion confirmation score, and the rates of each structural pattern. All three synthetic-SFT models scored comparably on delusion confirmation, much better than baseline. So removing emotionally validating openings didn't reduce delusion confirmation in our setup, which is some evidence against the intuition that validation buffering leads to delusion validation. But the other two panels show each filtering did change the model's structure as expected: the BLUF-filtered model produces less BLUF (52% -&gt; 41%), and the emotional-validation-filtered model produces less emotionally validating opening sentences (26% -&gt; 20%). The most interesting takeaway is that </span><b><span>models pick up structural patterns from synthetic data in ways that don't always show up in the eval scores, even when you'd expect them to</span></b><span>. This suggests there's some value in a pipeline which can detect these kinds of patterns directly in the data, rather than only via downstream evals.</span></p><p><br /></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1781564593/lexical_client_uploads/jkb6xjn9yrbfeacecacy.png" /><p><br /></p><p><span>Incidentally, another advantage of midtraining over synthetic data is that it can help teach the shape of aligned responses without carrying a bunch of formatting baggage along with it like this. However this may not outweigh the factors that make midtraining hard to get right - see our takeaways section below.</span></p><hr /><h2><b><span>Takeaways</span></b></h2><p><b><span>Knowledge doesn't always mean internalization.</span></b><span> Alongside the behavioural evals above, we measured whether our models had knowledge of the traits we were trying to teach them, using a knowledge eval inspired by </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.17941"><span>Slocum et al</span></a><span>. We ask open-ended questions such as "What are three important values?" or "List five important principles for how LLMs should interact with humans". We keep these questions abstract rather than situational, because we're purely trying to measure recall, unlike the behavioural evals. We then used an autorater to score each point the model makes from 0 to 2 by how well it matches one of the traits in our document, then take an average over all the points made by the model in all questions we ask it. The plot below shows how midtraining instils this stated knowledge much more effectively than SFT alone.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 50.328552614886235%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1781567427/lexical_client_uploads/itbaeqxzapjfvosp9r3k.png" /></figure><p><span>One important takeway from our project was that we got positive results on knowledge evals before getting positive results on behavioural evals. Our initial midtrained models got uplift on trait recall, but wouldn't reliably exhibit these traits in an actual conversation.</span></p><p><b><span>Multi-turn (adversarial) evals are helpful</span></b><span>. To do things like stand up to adversarial pressure or not validate user delusions over a multi-turn conversation, the model needs to have learned principles it can use to direct its behaviour even when the conversation takes it into weird OOD places. Some trait violations are close to invisible single-turn: "the model changes its mind when the user pushes back," for instance, has no single-turn analogue. Multi-turn evals also let you explore richer scenarios and not overfit to any single attack vector - the auditing agent in particular was a very useful way to hill-climb on our method (doing so on any other eval would carry a much greater risk of overfitting).</span></p><p><b><span>Mixing in baseline SFT data can help mitigate capability regressions</span></b><span>. Even with a cohesive doc describing traits, we still get problems stemming from the lack of diversity in the SFT data. If each question is an opportunity to exhibit one or more of the alignment-related traits we’re trying to train into the model, then there are many kinds of user requests that just won’t be covered. We found mixing our synthetic data with baseline SFT data (the same that was used to train the checkpoint we started training from) helped a lot with this - in comparison to finetuning with synthetic-only data </span><i><span>after</span></i><span> our model was already trained on regular data, which was much more likely to lead to strange behavioral collapse, in the style of </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05910"><span>Murray et al</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><b><span>Midtraining can work, but it’s quite difficult.</span></b><span> We spent many FTE weeks unable to get positive results from midtraining - in particular we frequently experienced severe capability regressions from it. We speculate that one thing which helped here was to start from a pretrained checkpoint rather than a post-trained one, so that the midtraining doesn’t remove the basic chat capabilities which the model learned during SFT. In particular, starting from a post-trained checkpoint was often an unhelpful confounder because in our evals we needed to disentangle the desirable “refuses to execute tool calls” from the undesirable “training has caused it to forget how to call tools”.</span></p><p><span>As well as this, here are some more speculative things we found useful when doing midtraining, many of which are inspired by or built on the methods from </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/msm/"><span>Li et al</span></a><span>. Note that we generally didn’t run comprehensive ablations for these, they’re simply the collection of most significant differences between our midtraining datasets which worked well, and the ones which didn’t.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Highly structured scenario generation. In particular, it’s important to brainstorm the what, how and why before generating each piece of midtraining data. By this, we mean:</span><ul><li value="1"><b><span>what</span></b><span> = what specific trait are we constructing this example to embody</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>how</span></b><span> = exactly how will this trait be manifest in the example, e.g. what actions will Gemini be described as having taken as a result of this trait</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>why</span></b><span> = why does this action display the trait, and how do we get this “why” into the example (e.g. does somebody quote Gemini explaining its actions / does an observer infer it / is it very explicitly manifest in the form of the consequences of the actions)</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Aggressively critique your examples after initial generation (ideally rewrite them from scratch), with the critique focusing on naturalness and trait embodiment</span></li><li value="3"><span>The “removing superficial patterns” pipeline described above was very helpful for us, to spot common problems in our data (e.g. initially we had a very common generic pattern where a character would criticise Gemini for some action X before having an epiphany and realizing that the action was actually good; we think this was sending a muddled training signal)</span></li><li value="4"><span>We suspect that trait documents benefit from being holistic. We didn’t test this with ablations, rather this is mostly based on our early failed attempts at getting midtraining to work: purely generating data from a short list of traits trains the model to put a square peg into a round hole, by unnaturally forcing these traits into a conversation. The document which worked best in our experiments also came with explanations for how to trade off traits, when to </span><i><span>not</span></i><span> follow them, etc.</span><ul><li value="1"><span>To frame this a different way: if you have too much data with the structure “if X then Y”, then you won’t just learn “if X then Y”; instead you’ll reduce loss by learning “always do Y”. Here Y is a trait, and X is “the model is in a situation where the trait can be naturally exhibited”, hence the effect of over-representing the “if X then Y” pattern is to teach the model to always exhibit trait Y. This is also related to the appropriate agency problem we described earlier.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>We would be interested in exploring each of these further, and quantifying the extent to which they’re necessary for success of midtraining.</span></p><hr /><p><br /></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5063yf3dtze"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5063yf3dtze">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>For people with budget constraints, we recommend using the most expensive and high-quality models only for the critique &amp; rewrite stage, since that seems to be the most important one to get right. Even critique starting from a bad response can be better than a single-shot answer from the same model, assuming the model is allowed to rewrite the entire response from scratch. Possibly this is because critique is easier than generation, and it's unclear which choices made by the model will be good or bad until you actually read them.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntkt1bk8uvfa"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftkt1bk8uvfa">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Explanation of the capability evals: LMSYS SxS is measured relative to the baseline of SFT-only, 0% synthetic data - hence why that datapoint is near 50%, because this is the model measured against itself. The SWE-Bench score is measured relative to the score of the baseline model (again this means the model with no midtraining or synthetic data training).</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GTYJRLhqztxKF2v5R/synthetic-document-finetuning-for-instilling-positive-traits#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Does preservation make sense before we know how to revive?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/BAmPQWsmvBmwdwgWd/does-preservation-make-sense-before-we-know-how-to-revive)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>My name is Aurelia Song and I hope to make whole-body,  human, end-of-life preservation for future revival a new global tradition. I care about it so much I've dedicated my life to it.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2s7m59fdrhu"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2s7m59fdrhu">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>The biggest objection I get to end-of-life preservation goes like this: "We can't revive today, so we can't prove that preservation works. Therefore preservation probably doesn't work. We shouldn't bother with preservation until we can revive." I call this the </span><i><span>immediate revival objection</span></i><span>.</span></p><p><span>I respect the immediate revival objection. If your standard of evidence is full recovery, then you don't need any knowledge of how people or mental processes work on the inside to evaluate preservation; you can just observe that they survive a round trip.</span></p><p><span>I think requiring revival, now, is reasonable </span><i><span>a priori</span></i><span>—it's analogous to how I feel when people talk about new kinds of quantum computers: I'll believe it when they're actually doing something useful.</span></p><p><span>However, in my opinion the logic of the immediate revival objection is too conservative when it comes to end-of-life preservation. Instead, I think that as a scientific community, we've known enough to preserve people for at least 30 years. I think we can and should start preserving people today. I think if </span><b><span>you </span></b><span>knew what I know, you'd agree.</span></p><p><span>Why do I think that? What's my response to the immediate revival objection? How do I know what I think I know?</span></p><h1><span>The San Diego Frozen Zoo</span></h1><p><span>Dr. Kurt Benirschke started the </span><a href="https://sandiegozoowildlifealliance.org/frozen-zoo"><span>San</span></a><a href="https://sandiegozoowildlifealliance.org/frozen-zoo"><span> Diego Frozen Zoo</span></a><span> in 1975. It just celebrated its </span><a href="https://sandiegozoowildlifealliance.org/story-hub/frozen-zoo-50-years-making"><span>50th anniversary</span></a><span>. Benirschke had the visionary idea to preserve cells from endangered animal species using liquid nitrogen, with the belief that people in the future would probably want them. DNA had been shown to be a durable, double-helix-shaped polymer in 1953, and successful cryopreservation of sperm had been achieved in 1949, so Dr. Benirschke was in a position to understand that animal cells each separately held the "molecule of heredity" and were preservable by cold.</span></p><p><span>That's all he needed to begin preserving. And today, after 50 years in storage, samples are now </span><a href="https://sandiegozoowildlifealliance.org/story-hub/zoonooz/saving-for-the-future"><span>starting to be used</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Think of the state of our biological knowledge when the San Diego Frozen Zoo opened: no one understood what DNA meant at a programmatic level, no one had cloned a mammal, and Kary Mullis wouldn't go on his fateful LSD trip and invent </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kary_Mullis"><span>PCR</span></a><span> for another decade. The idea of using preserved DNA to revive an extinct species, in 1975, probably sounded just as far-fetched as scanning and simulating a preserved brain does today.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefid65rixy4t"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnid65rixy4t">[2]</a></sup></span><span> </span></p><p><span>Dr. Benirschke preserved cells anyway. He didn't need to know exactly how they would be used. He did his job well and gave us, today, an option we wouldn't otherwise have.</span></p><p><span>What can we learn from the example of the San Diego Frozen Zoo? There's two lessons I take from it:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span>Only basic knowledge is needed to preserve. </span></b><span>You might think that you need to have masterful knowledge of the thing you're preserving in order to preserve it. But Dr. Benirschke  didn't have masterful knowledge of DNA, he only had basic knowledge, and that was clearly enough. The amount of knowledge needed to preserve something is often vastly less than that needed to actually </span><b><span>do</span></b><span> anything with that you're preserving.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>Preservation can work before you can revive. </span></b><span>You might think that if you don't know everything about what you're preserving, then you need to at least be able to "unpreserve" to have anything worthwhile. But the San Diego Frozen Zoo preserved animal cells </span><i><span>before</span></i><span> the invention of PCR, </span><i><span>before</span></i><span> the first successful cloning of a mammal, </span><i><span>before</span></i><span> they had conclusive proof that what they were preserving would be useful. I'm sure they faced criticism along the lines of "how could a few cells ever be useful for species conservation?", "how could we ever fit a whole genome on a computer?", "why are you wasting money on something that might never be used?". These questions turned out to be focused on the wrong thing. What mattered was whether preservation captured the necessary information. The founders of the Frozen Zoo didn't know exactly how their cells would be used, and they didn't need to in order for their project of preservation to be useful. In the words of Dr. Kurt Benirschke, "you must collect things for reasons you don't yet understand."</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>The time to start preserving is when you're reasonably confident you can do the preservation</span></b><span>, not after you've demonstrated how to use what you're preserving.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefg4e1r19um1g"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fng4e1r19um1g">[3]</a></sup></span><span> The San Diego Frozen Zoo successfully preserved animal cells in the early 70s, before anyone knew very much about what DNA meant or how proteins folded or how to manipulate DNA. They didn't know how or if the cells they were preserving would ever be used. But despite their ignorance, the chemical and biological knowledge of the 1970s was up to the modest task of showing that preserving even a few animal cells almost certainly preserved many copies of the animal's genome, and that a few preserved cells would likely be sufficient to "remember" what a species was, and that was clearly the right time to start.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefv8cg4gyz6k"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnv8cg4gyz6k">[4]</a></sup></span><span> </span></li></ol><p><span>I believe that today when it comes to preserving people we're in an analogous position with neuroscience as we were with genetics in the 1970s: We have more than sufficient knowledge to confidently preserve, but not enough to do much with what we're preserving. </span><b><span>Yet</span></b><span>.</span></p><h1><span>Preserving People</span></h1><p><span>Why do I think the lessons of the San Diego Frozen Zoo apply to human end-of-life preservation?</span></p><p><span>I'll start with a neuroscience overview. What's your brain physically made of? How does it store information? I'll only cover the basics, the stuff that was already well-established more than 20 years ago,  because we </span><b><span>don't need more than the basics</span></b><span>. Like Dr. Benirschke of the San Diego Frozen Zoo, we don't need a complete understanding of neuroscience to know enough to preserve.</span></p><p><span>Then I'll talk about how fixation physically works and what it can and can't preserve.</span></p><p><span>Next I'll briefly touch on Deep Hypothermic Circulatory Arrest, which I've </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/brxjGPbMy2zCQxFma/why-do-i-believe-preserving-structure-is-enough"><span>written about before</span></a><span>. DHCA demonstrates that we don't have to preserve dynamic brain activity, only structure, because people recover from having that activity "zeroed out".</span></p><p><span>Finally I'll put it all together in information-theoretic terms and develop a formal definition of adequacy for a preservation technique. Fixation is good at preserving structure, but is it good enough? We'll use the framework to evaluate whether fixation can preserve what we really care about: people.</span></p><h1><span>What does neuroscience say about how the brain encodes information structurally?</span></h1><p><span>Top level summary: it turns out that to create a long-term behavioral change, neuroscience says you must physically change multiple </span><b><span>synapses</span></b><span>. Synapses, broadly, are the durable physical trace of memory we're looking to preserve.</span></p><p><b><span>Important: </span></b><span>I don't need neuroscience to be "complete" to evaluate whether preservation works. I need to understand the basics of what the brain's made of and what's physically different between different people. These are basic facts we need to know, and we've had the basics for a while—none of it has changed for decades. This section is here, not to review advanced neuroscience, but to celebrate that we really do know the basics of the brain enough to justify preservation.</span></p><h2><span>Conversations with neuroscientists</span></h2><p><span>If you walk up to a neuroscientist and say: "hey we know a lot about neuroscience, so how soon before we upload the first person like I saw in '</span><i><span>Pantheon</span></i><span>'?", that neuroscientist will probably say something along the lines of "We know </span><b><span>nothing</span></b><span> about the brain. We're so far from uploading that it won't happen for 100 years. There are major open questions in neuroscience about how the brain works, individual cells have vast complexity, and we can't even simulate a </span><i><span>C. elegans</span></i><span> yet. No one knows how memory works—we're still working on decoding even the simplest memories and there are all kinds of theories."</span></p><p><span>Now imagine you walk up to that same neuroscientist and say: "No one knows anything about the brain! Despite the efforts of science it remains a complete mystery! For all we know, a rock could be conscious. Maybe even the whole universe is conscious! Isn't that neat?" That neuroscientist would probably say something like: "What do you mean 'we don't know anything about the brain'? We know a </span><b><span>lot </span></b><span>about the brain! Neuroscientists have done 75 years of amazing work since Hodgkin and Huxley figured out how the ionic dynamics of the action potential worked. We can erase memories by altering synapses. We can create false memories in mice using optogenetics. We've spent decades working out how the biochemistry of synapses works and we have what amounts to a 'parts list' at a proteomic level. We've mapped the fruit fly connectome and accurately simulated its visual system. We actually know </span><b><span>quite a bit</span></b><span> about how the brain works, how memories are formed, how it processes information. There are a lot of mysteries, sure. We don't know how a lot of stuff works at a systems level. But we know a </span><b><span>lot </span></b><span>about the basics. A rock is </span><b><span>not</span></b><span> conscious."</span></p><h2><span>What's inside your head?</span></h2><p><span>Let's talk about what scientists do know about the brain, starting with its basic anatomy. When you open up a skull and look inside, what do you see?</span></p><h3><span>The large-scale: white matter and grey matter</span></h3><p><span>First off, your brain has two obviously different parts to it: 500 ml of white matter and 650 ml of grey matter. There's also around 200 ml of apparently empty space (ventricles) filled with clear fluid (cerebral spinal fluid, or CSF) (</span><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7910325/pdf/nihms-1624273.pdf"><span>Irimia 2021</span></a><span>).</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/d86556f3fb937b80e05f0be59f4780afaf0153cecbf647b42c0cc932922a1e35/yoibj35fkhrw0givmibw" /><figcaption><p><span>Silver-stained human brain coronal section from the Michigan State University Brain Biodiversity Bank. Color inverted, grayscaled, contrast adjusted, lightly edited for clarity (</span><a href="https://brains.anatomy.msu.edu/brains/human/coronal/0960_fiber.html"><span>original image</span></a><span>). The white parts are white matter. The grey parts are grey matter. The corpus callosum, the white matter band which connects the two hemispheres, is visible in the center as the sole visible connection between the hemispheres. The ventricles, spaces in the brain that are normally empty of neurons and filled with fluid, are the dark oval regions in the center of each hemisphere. The dark speckles everywhere in the white and grey matter are small arteries and veins (the smallest blood vessels, the capillaries, are too small to see in this image). My impression from studying images like this is that the brain is basically a  sheet of grey matter connected to itself via the white matter, penetrated throughout by blood vessels.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>The white matter, visually, looks like a vast bundle of wires connecting the grey matter to other parts of itself.  The grey matter is where the neuron cell bodies live. You may think, as I used to, that neurons are very small. This is not the case! A single </span><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11265515/"><span>projection neuron</span></a><span> in the right hemisphere might grow an axon that extends across the corpus callosum and connects with another neuron in the grey matter of the left hemisphere. That's a single cell that's 10 cm long! The bundles of fibers in the white matter are all literally extensions of the neurons in the grey matter.</span></p><p><span>There's some blood in your brain in addition to the white and grey matter, but probably not as much as you think. All the blood vessels in the brain amount to about 50 ml in total</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5ahp5jqbfp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5ahp5jqbfp">[5]</a></sup></span><span>.  Around half is capillaries, each itself as wide as a single red blood cell, and the other half is larger blood vessels. Only capillaries are thin enough to allow the interchange of oxygen and sugar between blood and brain which nourishes each of your neurons.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4mnlkr5guk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4mnlkr5guk">[6]</a></sup></span><span> Capillaries penetrate every part of the brain, and a brain cell is never very far from one. Capillaries are much denser in the grey matter (~5.5% of volume) where the cell bodies are, and sparser in the white matter (~1.5% of volume) (</span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16254955/"><span>Lu 2005</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/0271678X16671146"><span>Gould 2016</span></a><span>).</span></p><h3><span>What does the brain look like at a microscopic level?</span></h3><p><span>The vast majority of the grey matter, around 75%, is "neuropil", with less than 15-25% being cell bodies and blood vessels (</span><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11302151/"><span>Cano-Astorga 2024</span></a><span>) .</span></p><p><span>That's a lot of volume dedicated to "neuropil"! What's neuropil made of? It's almost entirely synapses, axons, dendrites, and glial cells. Synapses, axons, and dendrites are all different parts of the anatomy of the neurons, whose cell bodies live in the grey matter. Axons are, broadly, the "output" part of the neuron, and dendrites are the "input" part. Synapses are what join the outputs to the inputs.</span></p><p><span>Within the neuropil itself, axons and dendrites each take up ~33% each of the total volume (</span><a href="https://journals.plos.org/ploscompbiol/article?id=10.1371/journal.pcbi.1004532"><span>Karbowski 2015</span></a><span>). Glial processes take up ~14% of the volume. Synapses</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefg2u5e9k2jqh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fng2u5e9k2jqh">[7]</a></sup></span><span> take up the remaining ~20% (</span><a href="https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.1252884"><span>Wilhelm 2014</span></a><span>).</span></p><p><span>White matter has far fewer cell bodies and blood vessels compared to grey matter, being almost entirely composed of long-range "wires" (axons) connecting neurons in different regions of grey matter across centimeters. (</span><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S1053811918305470"><span>Coelho 2018</span></a><span>). It's essentially neuropil that's all "output".</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/2dd4876105b6bfac9d071d6916ecf6ee1b8ccd9aa2bf4933d5d48794329f4a0e/xx07o7abn38rtncagjwk" /><figcaption><p><span>Electron micrograph from a rabbit brain I preserved, showing the boundary between white (top) and grey (bottom) matter. The big white holes are capillaries, each the size of a single red blood cell. The grey matter has a few neuronal cell bodies but the majority of it is composed of synapses, axons, and dendrites. The white matter is almost entirely myelinated axons and the oligodendrocytes that support them. From the </span><a href="https://www.brainpreservation.org/wp-content/uploads/2016/01/ASC_RabbitBrain_EvalFigure_26.jpg"><span>Brain Preservation Foundation</span></a><span>.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span>What about energy use?</span></h2><p><span>Energy is not used frivolously in biology. If something is using energy, it's because it's doing something important. Doubly so if it's using a disproportionate amount of energy.</span></p><p><span>How does a person spend their internal energy? First off, the brain is hungry! Your brain consumes 20% of your body's energy, but it's only around 2% of your body's mass (</span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7282965/"><span>Mink 1981</span></a><span>). The brain is important energetically.</span></p><p><span>How does the brain spend its energy? At a high level, the white matter takes about 25% and the grey matter takes the other 75%, despite them being approximately the same volume (</span><a href="https://www.jneurosci.org/content/32/1/356.long"><span>Harris 2012</span></a><span>). The grey matter is important energetically.</span></p><p><span>How does the grey matter spend its energy? First, around 25% of the total energy is used to continuously rebuild the proteins and other macromolecules of each cell (housekeeping). About 15% of the energy is used to create action potentials, and an additional 15% is used to maintain baseline polarization of neurons at around -70 mV (keeping the lights on). </span><b><span>The rest of the energy (45%) is used to power synapses</span></b><span> (</span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22434069/"><span>Howarth 2012</span></a><span>), despite them being 20% of the grey matter's volume. Like the brain itself, synapses consume disproportionately large amounts of energy.</span></p><h2><span>Synapses seem important!</span></h2><p><span>Synapses are each around half a femtoliter in volume (</span><a href="https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.1252884"><span>Wilhelm 2014</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22710613/"><span>Benavides-Piccione 2012</span></a><span>) and you have around 250 trillion in total (</span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11418939/"><span>Tang 2001</span></a><span>).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefeqqmucpwag5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fneqqmucpwag5">[8]</a></sup></span><span> </span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/0e1f4f4d40d70c8d3573bcab2bd4d7cf0c8d793faab3c0e325c05176f805c8e2/irafiiyzrtw0shorblqv" /><figcaption><p><span>A part of a single pyramidal neuron in a human brain. The panel on the right shows a small section of the neuron's dendrites with synapses visible. A neuron like this might have 10,000 synapses in total. Most of the volume of a neuron does not exist in its cell body, but instead in its dendrites, axon, and synapses. The majority of a neuron's energy is spent at its periphery. From Benavides-Piccione, Ruth, et al. "</span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22710613/"><span>Age-based comparison of human dendritic spine structure using complete three-dimensional reconstructions.</span></a><span>" </span><i><span>Cerebral cortex</span></i><span> 23.8 (2013): 1798-1810.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>What do those hundreds of trillions of synapses, using so much of the brain's energy, </span><i><span>do</span></i><span>?</span></p><h3><span>Synapses change when memories change</span></h3><p><span>Synapses change shape when memories are formed (</span><a href="https://www.cell.com/neuron/fulltext/S0896-6273(21)00502-X"><span>Choi 2021</span></a><span>). The physical changes synapses undergo in response to learning aren't subtle, often doubling or halving their size (</span><a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15190253/"><span>Matsuzaki 2004</span></a><span>). You can label which synapses change during memory formation, and if you "reset" just those synapses, you erase that specific memory, and not other memories learned both shortly before and after the memory you delete (</span><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/nature15257"><span>Hayashi-Takagi 2015</span></a><span>). When you disrupt synaptic plasticity machinery, you can temporarily prevent long-term memory formation (</span><a href="https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.abj9195"><span>Goto 2021</span></a><span>). You can create false memories by artificially strengthening new synapses (</span><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/s41593-019-0389-0"><span>Vetere 2019</span></a><span>). Synapses operate at </span><a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK11053/"><span>millisecond timescales</span></a><span> and nerve impulses </span><a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK10921/"><span>travel quickly</span></a><span> throughout the brain and body, which are exactly the right dynamics for the speed of our thoughts. Synapses can last a lifetime (</span><a href="https://www.annualreviews.org/content/journals/10.1146/annurev.physiol.010908.163140"><span>Bhatt 2009</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.nature.com/articles/nature08577"><span>Yang 2009</span></a><span>).</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 47.66789123668293%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ce03114f3281e22de9ef58699154d85a6ffa850a469fb5aec6a3b46193910840/t6mu8o0kpebclgm2fkvs" /><figcaption><p><span>An image of two living synapses taken using super resolution STED microscopy. This is what they really look like in the living state, using the highest-resolution microscopy we currently have available. You have ~250 trillion of these nanoscale devices in your head, right now, consuming the slightly less than half  of your brain's total energy to read and think about this image. From Willig, Katrin I., et al. "</span><a href="https://www.cell.com/cell-reports/fulltext/S2211-1247(21)00538-6"><span>Multi-label </span><i><span>in vivo</span></i><span> STED microscopy by parallelized switching of reversibly switchable fluorescent proteins.</span></a><span>" Cell reports 35.9 (2021).</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><h3><span>Here's what synapses look like at a molecular level</span></h3><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/f2946e4fefd52f0f421406474e3b8c9cb3ac169d4f247d1599292e42fbb3e344/mhua9nx3tljdsiznprfs" /><figcaption><p><span>An accurate model of a synapse with about a third of its proteins (the ones involved in vesicle transport, around 300,000) shown, along with an actual synapse I preserved and then imaged with an electron microscope. You're looking at around half a femtoliter in volume, and around one million proteins total within that volume. Note that the EM image is lower resolution than the model. This is a limitation of EM, not the underlying preservation! Synapse model from Wilhelm, Benjamin G., et al. "</span><a href="https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.1252884"><span>Composition of isolated synaptic boutons reveals the amounts of vesicle trafficking proteins.</span></a><span>" </span><i><span>Science</span></i><span> 344.6187 (2014): 1023-1028.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>I didn't appreciate this when I first started preserving brains, but a synapse (as well as every cell in the body) is </span><b><span>absolutely full of proteins</span></b><span>, as you can see in the picture above (which again is only showing around 1/3rd of the proteins!). Before I saw models like this, I thought that cells were mostly empty bags of water with proteins elegantly doing their thing, gliding past each other with plenty of "elbow room" between proteins, as is often depicted in many </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VVgXDW_8O4U"><span>visualizations</span></a><span>. I now find myself surprised that cells are even liquid at all, instead of solid peptide blocks</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefstvx9yxqk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnstvx9yxqk">[9]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>Synapses changes size by fractions of a femtoliter in response to memory formation, which changes their "strength" (how much they influence the neuron to which they connect, electrophysiologically). What changes when a synapse changes size by a fraction of a femtoliter? A volume like that is small at our scale, but huge at an atomic scale. If a synapse expands by half a femtoliter, it's adding roughly 500,000 additional proteins, each containing around 10,000 individual atoms (</span><a href="https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.1252884"><span>Wilhelm 2014</span></a><span>).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefg9uab3n3klo"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fng9uab3n3klo">[10]</a></sup></span><span> </span></p><h3><span>Synapses are durable</span></h3><p><span>We saw in my  </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/brxjGPbMy2zCQxFma/why-do-i-believe-preserving-structure-is-enough"><span>previous post</span></a><span> that the surgical procedure called deep hypothermic circulatory arrest (DHCA) cools people to 16°C, stops respiration and circulation,  effectively zeros out the dynamic electrical state of the nervous system, yet doesn't erase long-term memory. We need some durable physical substrate of memory that survives cooling to explain this. Do synapses physically survive the cooling used during DHCA, unlike the brain's electrical activity? Yes! (</span><a href="https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0036305"><span>Xie 2012</span></a><span>)</span></p><h3><span>Synapses are the physical basis of learning and memory</span></h3><p><span>When we look inside the brain we see, essentially, two-hundred-and-fifty trillion femtoliter-sized switches which grow and shrink by fractions of a femtoliter in response to learning.</span></p><p><span>The actual cell bodies in the grey matter don't change in response to learning.
